on the incoming edges of a 6-valent vertex
v
, and weights
a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},a^{\prime}_{3}

(
resp.
b^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{2},b^{\prime}_{3})
on the outgoing edges. By definition, the local intersection number of
C,C^{\prime}
at
v
is

\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}\\
\end{matrix}\right|-\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
a^{\prime}_{1}&a^{\prime}_{2}&a^{\prime}_{3}\\
b^{\prime}_{1}&b^{\prime}_{2}&b^{\prime}_{3}\\
\end{matrix}\right|\right).

Subsection
11.1
provides an example computing these local intersections for cycles with weights 0 and 1.

Remark 4.19
.

See Section
7.4
for one of the geometric motivations behind these definitions. In particular, Lemma
7.17
asserts that for
\mathsf{G}=\SL_{n}
the formulas in Definitions
4.17
and
4.18
compute actual intersection numbers between 1-dimensional homology classes on surfaces. This case was first studied in
[
20
, Section 2]
, cf. also
[
19
, Section 3]
. Figures
24
and
25
in Section
7.4
illustrate how to associate a curve to a Lusztig cycle, and Figures
27
and
28
depict some of their geometric intersections.

Example 4.20
.

Let
C,C^{\prime}
be Lusztig cycles. Suppose
C
has weights
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=(1,0,0)
on the top of
v
. Therefore
(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},a^{\prime}_{3})=(0,0,1)
. Then their local intersection at
v
is

\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\left((b_{2}-b_{3})-(b^{\prime}_{1}-b^{\prime}_{2})\right).

Since
b^{\prime}_{1}=b_{2}+b_{3}-b^{\prime}_{2}
, we get

\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=b^{\prime}_{2}-b_{3}
, and
\sharp_{v}(C^{\prime}\cdot C)=b_{3}-b^{\prime}_{2}.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a weave and a 6-valent vertex
v\in\mathfrak{W}
. Consider three Lusztig cycles
C,C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
whose weights are
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
,
(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})
and
(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})
on the top of
v
. Then the following holds:

(1)

If
(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})=(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})+(1,0,1)
then
\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime\prime})
.

(2)

If
(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})=(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})+(0,1,0)
then
\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof.

For
(1)
, we have
\min(c_{1},c_{3})=\min(b_{1},b_{3})+1
and Lusztig’s rules in Definition
4.9
imply
(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2},c^{\prime}_{3})=(b^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{2},b^{\prime}_{3})+(0,1,0)
. Consider the cycle
\overline{C}
with weights
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(1,0,1)
above
v
, and weights
(d^{\prime}_{1},d^{\prime}_{2},d^{\prime}_{3})=(0,1,0)
below
v
, which is a Lusztig cycle. Then
C^{\prime\prime}=C^{\prime}+\overline{C}
, addition here being understood as adding the upper weights with upper weights and adding lower weights with lower weights. In consequence,
\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime\prime})=\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})+\sharp_{v}(C\cdot\overline{C})
, since determinants are multilinear. It thus suffices to compute
\sharp_{v}(C\cdot\overline{C})
, which is

\sharp_{v}(C\cdot\overline{C})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
1&0&1\\
\end{matrix}\right|-\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
a^{\prime}_{1}&a^{\prime}_{2}&a^{\prime}_{3}\\
0&1&0\\
\end{matrix}\right|\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left[(a_{3}-a_{1})-(a^{\prime}_{1}-a^{\prime}_{3})\right]=

=\frac{1}{2}\left[(a_{3}-a_{1})-((a_{2}+a_{3}-\min(a_{1},a_{3}))-(a_{2}+a_{1}-\min(a_{1},a_{3})))\right]=0.

Here we have used that
C
is a Lusztig cycle to express
a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{3}
in terms of
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
. From this computation we conclude that
\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime\prime})=\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})+\sharp_{v}(C\cdot\overline{C})=\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})
, as required. The proof of
(2)
is similar.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

Consider the notation of Lemma
4.21
. We can write the top weights
(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})
of the Lusztig cycle
C^{\prime\prime}
as a positive linear combination of
(1,0,1)
,
(0,1,0)
and one of either
(1,0,0)
or
(0,0,1)
:

(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})=c_{3}\cdot(1,0,1)+c_{2}\cdot(0,1,0)+(c_{1}-c_{3})\cdot(1,0,0),\qquad\mbox{if $c_{3}\leq c_{1}$},\quad\mbox{or}

(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})=c_{1}\cdot(1,0,1)+c_{2}\cdot(0,1,0)+(c_{3}-c_{1})\cdot(0,0,1),\qquad\mbox{if $c_{1}\leq c_{3}$}.

The local intersection
\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime\prime})
at the hexavalent vertex
v
of any Lusztig cycle
C
with
C^{\prime\prime}
can then be simplified by using Lemma
4.21
. Indeed, iteratively using the lemma, the computation of
\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime\prime})
is reduced to computing the local intersection numbers of
C
with Lusztig cycles that have weights
(c,0,0)
or
(0,0,c)
at the top, for some natural number
c
. In particular, following Example
4.20
, we conclude that the local interesection number of Lusztig cycles at a 6-valent vertex is always an integer number.

Definition 4.23
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a weave and
C,C^{\prime}:E(\mathfrak{W})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
cycles. By definition, the intersection number
\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}}(C\cdot C^{\prime})
of
C
and
C^{\prime}
is

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}}(C\cdot C^{\prime}):=\sum_{v\,3\text{-valent}}\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime})+\sum_{v\,6\text{-valent}}\sharp_{v}(C\cdot C^{\prime}).

Note that
\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=-\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}}(C^{\prime}\cdot C)
and that
\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}}(C\cdot C^{\prime})
is an integer when
C,C^{\prime}
are both Lusztig cycles.

4.6.
Quiver from local intersections

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a Demazure weave. Definition
4.23
, along with the following notion of boundary intersections in Definition
4.24
, allow us to associate a quiver
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
to
\mathfrak{W}
.

Recall that we denote the Cartan subgroup of
\mathsf{G}
by
T
. Denote by
X
and
X^{\vee}
the lattices of characters and cocharacters of
T
. Consider the perfect pairing

(15)

(\cdot,\cdot):~X\times X^{\vee}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}.

Let
\{\alpha_{i}\}
and
\{\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\}
be the set of simple roots and simple coroots, indexed by the vertices in
\mathsf{D}
.
Now given a braid word
\beta=\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{k}}
, we consider the following sequences of roots and coroots (cf.
[
21
]
):

(16)

\rho_{j}:=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{j-1}}(\alpha_{i_{j}}),\qquad\rho_{j}^{\vee}:=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{j-1}}(\alpha_{i_{j}}^{\vee}),\qquad\forall 1\leq j\leq k.

Note that
\rho_{j}=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{j}}(-\alpha_{i_{j}})
. For a weave
\mathfrak{W}
and a cycle
C
, such that
\beta
appears as a horizontal section of
\mathfrak{W}
, we denote by
c_{j}
the weight of
C
on the
j
-th letter of
\beta
.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a weave and
C,C^{\prime}:E(\mathfrak{W})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
cycles and
\beta=\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{r}}
a braid word which is a horizontal section of
\mathfrak{W}
. By definition, the boundary intersection
\sharp_{\beta}(C\cdot C^{\prime})
of
C,C^{\prime}
at
\beta
is

\sharp_{\beta}(C\cdot C^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}\sign(j-i)c_{i}c^{\prime}_{j}\cdot(\rho_{i},\rho_{j}^{\vee})

where
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the pairing defined via (
15
), and

\sign(k)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }k>0,\\
0&\mbox{if }k=0,\\
-1&\mbox{if }k<0.\end{cases}

Remark 4.25
.

In Definition
4.24
and throughout this section we are assuming that the group
\mathsf{G}
is of simply laced type. For non-simply laced type Definition
4.24
has to be modified to take into account cycles for the Langlands dual group
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
of
\mathsf{G}
, see Section
6.1
and in particular (
38
) below.

Definition 4.26
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a Demazure weave. By definition, the quiver
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
is the quiver whose vertices are (in bijective correspondence with) the trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{W}
, and whose adjacency matrix is given by

\varepsilon_{v,v^{\prime}}:=\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}}(\gamma_{v}\cdot\gamma_{v^{\prime}})+\sharp_{\delta(\beta)}(\gamma_{v}\cdot\gamma_{v^{\prime}}).

where
\delta(\beta)
is the bottom slice of the weave
\mathfrak{W}
.

Remark 4.27
.

The entries
\varepsilon_{v,v^{\prime}}
in Definition
4.26
are always half-integers but not necessarily integers. Note also that the boundary intersection terms for
\varepsilon_{v,v^{\prime}}
vanish for cycles
\gamma_{v},\gamma_{v^{\prime}}
(either of) which do not reach the bottom part of the weave: in the language of Subsection
4.7
, the boundary intersection terms only appear between frozen vertices, and the weights of arrows involving a mutable vertex are always integers.

Let us now continue our study of
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
and its dependence on the weave
\mathfrak{W}
.

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a weave with no trivalent vertices. Then for any two Lusztig cycles
C,C^{\prime}
the sum of local intersection numbers equals the difference of boundary intersection numbers.

Proof.

It suffices to verify this for a single 6-valent vertex and a single
4
-valent vertex, which are local computations. For the former, suppose that the Lusztig cycle
C
has weights
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
on top of a
6
-valent vertex, while the Lusztig cycle
C^{\prime}
has weights
(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})
, also on top. By Lemma
4.21
, we can assume that
a_{2}=a_{3}=b_{2}=b_{1}=0
, so the intersection number around the
6
-valent vertex is
\sharp(C\cdot C^{\prime})=-a_{1}b_{3}
. We may also assume that the roots at the top boundary are
\rho_{3}=\alpha_{j}
,
\rho_{2}=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}
and
\rho_{1}=\alpha_{i}
where
i,j\in\mathsf{D}
are adjacent. Thus, the top intersection number is

\#_{\mathrm{top}}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\sign(3-1)a_{1}b_{3}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})=-\frac{1}{2}a_{1}b_{3}

and the bottom intersection number is

\#_{\mathrm{bottom}}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\sign(1-3)a^{\prime}_{3}b^{\prime}_{1}(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=\frac{1}{2}a_{1}b_{3}.

The result for
6
-valent vertices thus follows. For a
4
-valent vertex, suppose that we have Lusztig cycles
C
,
C^{\prime}
with weights
a_{1},a_{2}
and
b_{1},b_{2}
at the top, respectively. Then the top boundary intersection number is
a_{1}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{1}
, while the bottom boundary intersection number is
a^{\prime}_{1}b^{\prime}_{2}-a^{\prime}_{2}b^{\prime}_{1}=a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2}
. Thus, the difference between the boundary intersection numbers is
0
, as required.
∎

Corollary 4.29
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}_{1},\mathfrak{W}_{2}:\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
be two weaves with no trivalent vertices. Suppose that
C_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}},C^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}
are Lusztig cycles in
\mathfrak{W}_{1}
,
C_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}},C^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}
are Lusztig cycles in
\mathfrak{W}_{2}
, and the initial weights of
C_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}

(
resp.
C^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}})
are the same as those of
C_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}

(
resp.
C^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}})
. Then
\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C\cdot C^{\prime})=\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C\cdot C^{\prime}).

Proof.

Indeed, both intersection numbers are equal to
\sharp_{\beta}(C\cdot C^{\prime})-\sharp_{\beta^{\prime}}(C\cdot C^{\prime}).

Note that by Lemma
4.10
the output weights of
C_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}

(
resp.
C^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}})
are the same as those of
C_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}

(
resp.
C^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}})
.
∎

Corollary
4.29
implies that if two weaves
\mathfrak{W},\mathfrak{W}^{\prime}
that are equivalent via an equivalence that uses only
4
- and
6
-valent vertices, then the corresponding quivers
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
and
Q_{\mathfrak{W}^{\prime}}
coincide. Let us now prove the stronger result that any two equivalent weaves yield the same quiver. For that, it suffices to study weave equivalences that involve
3
-valent vertices, which locally are those in Example
4.12
, see Figure
4
.

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}_{1}:1212\to 2122\to 212
and
\mathfrak{W}_{2}:1212\to 1121\to 121\to 212
and
C_{i},C^{\prime}_{i}
be Lusztig cycles on
\mathfrak{W}_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Suppose that the initial weights of
C_{1}

(
resp.
C^{\prime}_{1})
coincide with those of
C_{2}

(
resp.
C^{\prime}_{2})
, and let
\gamma^{i}_{v}
be the cycle originating at the unique trivalent vertex of
\mathfrak{W}_{i}
. Then we have the equalities:

(1)

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C_{1},\gamma^{1}_{v})=\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C_{2},\gamma^{2}_{v})

(2)

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C_{1},C^{\prime}_{1})=\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C_{2},C^{\prime}_{2})

Proof.

For (1), we follow the notations of Example
4.12
, so
C_{1},C_{2}
have weights
a,b,c,d
on the top. For the weave
\mathfrak{W}_{1}
, the only local intersection is at trivalent vertex and thus

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C_{1},\gamma_{v}^{1})=a+b-\min(a,c)-d.

For
\mathfrak{W}_{1}
, the local intersection at trivalent vertex equals
a-c-d+\min(b,d)
, while the local intersection at the bottom 6-valent vertex equals

b+c-\min(b,d)-\min(a,c)
, as in Example
4.20
. By combining these together we also obtain

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(G,G_{v})=(a-c-d+\min(b,d))+(b+c-\min(b,d)-\min(a,c))=a+b-\min(a,c)-d.

For (2), Lemma
4.21
, implies that adding
(1,0,1,0)
and
(0,1,0,1)
on top of either weave does not change the intersection number at any vertex of either weave. Thus we assume that
C_{i}
and
C^{\prime}_{i}
have weights
(a,0,0,b)
and
(c,0,0,d)
on top, where
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that
a,b,c,d
could be negative here. Denote
m_{ab}:=\min([a]_{+},b)
and
m_{cd}:=\min([c]_{+},d)
and let us compute the intersection numbers for
\mathfrak{W}_{1}
. At the 6-valent vertex we have

\frac{1}{2}\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
a&0&0\\
c&0&0\\
\end{matrix}\right|-\frac{1}{2}\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
-[a]_{-}&[a]_{-}&[a]_{+}\\
-[c]_{-}&[c]_{-}&[c]_{+}\\
\end{matrix}\right|=[a]_{+}[c]_{-}-[a]_{-}[c]_{+}.

At the 3-valent vertex we have

\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
[a]_{+}&m_{ab}&b\\
[c]_{+}&m_{cd}&d\\
\end{matrix}\right|=m_{ab}([d]_{+}+[d]_{-}-[c]_{+})+m_{cd}([a]_{+}-[b]_{+}-[b]_{-})+

[b]_{+}[c]_{+}+[b]_{-}[c]_{+}-[a]_{+}[d]_{+}-[a]_{+}[d]_{-}.

The intersection numbers for
\mathfrak{W}_{2}
are as follows; at the top 6-valent vertex we have

\frac{1}{2}\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
0&0&b\\
0&0&d\\
\end{matrix}\right|-\frac{1}{2}\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
[b]_{+}&[b]_{-}&-[b]_{-}\\
[d]_{+}&[d]_{-}&-[d]_{-}\\
\end{matrix}\right|=[b]_{-}[d]_{+}-[b]_{+}[d]_{-}.

At the 3-valent vertex we have:

\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
a&m_{ab}+[a]_{-}-[b]_{-}&[b]_{+}\\
c&m_{cd}+[c]_{-}-[d]_{-}&[d]_{+}\\
\end{matrix}\right|=m_{ab}([d]_{+}-[c]_{+}-[c]_{-})+m_{cd}([a]_{+}+[a]_{-}-[b]_{+})+

-[b]_{-}[d]_{+}+[b]_{+}[c]_{+}-[a]_{-}[d]_{-}+[a]_{+}[c]_{-}-[a]_{+}[d]_{-}-[a]_{-}[c]_{+}+[b]_{-}[c]_{-}+[b]_{-}[c]_{+}+[b]_{+}[d]_{-}-[a]_{+}[d]_{+},

where we have used the equality
\min(a,[b]_{+})=\min([a]_{+},b)+[a]_{-}-[b]_{-}
. Finally, at the bottom 6-valent vertex we have

\frac{1}{2}\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
m_{ab}+[a]_{-}-[b]_{-}&[b]_{-}&-[b]_{-}\\
m_{cd}+[c]_{-}-[d]_{-}&[d]_{-}&-[d]_{-}\\
\end{matrix}\right|-\frac{1}{2}\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
-[a]_{-}&[a]_{-}&m_{ab}\\
-[c]_{-}&[c]_{-}&m_{cd}\\
\end{matrix}\right|=m_{ab}([d]_{-}+[c]_{-})-m_{cd}([b]_{-}+[a]_{-})+

[a]_{-}[d]_{-}-[b]_{-}[c]_{-}.

By adding these local intersection indices, we obtain
\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C_{1},C^{\prime}_{1})=\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C_{2},C^{\prime}_{2})
as required.
∎

Corollary 4.31
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}_{1},\mathfrak{W}_{2}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be two equivalent weaves, where the same braid word has been fixed for
\delta(\beta)
. Then the quivers
Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}
and
Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}
coincide.

Let us now study the effect that weave mutation has on the associated quivers. We use the following:

Lemma 4.32
.

Let
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}
, then the following two identities hold:

(1)

[b-a+\min(a,b,c)-c]_{-}=-[a+c-b-\min(a,b,c)]_{+}=\min(a,b)-c-a+\min(b,c).

(2)

[b-a+\min(a,b,c)-c]_{+}=-[a+c-b-\min(a,b,c)]_{-}=b+\min(a,b,c)-\min(a,b)-\min(b,c).

Proof.

Part (1) is a tropicalization of the identity

1+\frac{t^{b}(t^{a}+t^{b}+t^{c})}{t^{a}t^{c}}=\frac{(t^{a}+t^{b})(t^{b}+t^{c})}{t^{a}t^{c}},

and (2) follows by

[(b-a)+(\min(a,b,c)-c),0]_{+}+[(b-a)+(\min(a,b,c)-c)]_{-}=(b-a)+(\min(a,b,c)-c).

∎

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}_{1},\mathfrak{W}_{2}
be the two Demazure weaves for
\sigma_{1}^{3}
depicted in Figure
5
. Consider the following three types of cycles:
C,C^{\prime}
are Lusztig cycles with initial weights
a,b,c
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}
;
\gamma_{v_{1}}
is the short cycle connecting the trivalent vertices;
\gamma_{v_{2}}
is the cycle exiting the bottom trivalent vertex. Then:

(1)

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})=-\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})
.

(2)

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(\gamma_{v_{1}},\gamma_{v_{2}})=1,\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(\gamma_{v_{1}},\gamma_{v_{2}})=-1.

(3)

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C,\gamma_{v_{2}})=\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{2}})+\left[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})\right]_{+}=\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{2}})-\left[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})\right]_{+}\left[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(\gamma_{v_{2}},\gamma_{v_{1}})\right]_{-}.

(4)

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C,C^{\prime})=\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,C^{\prime})-[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})]_{+}[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C^{\prime},\gamma_{v_{1}})]_{-}+[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})]_{-}[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C^{\prime},\gamma_{v_{1}})]_{+}.

Proof.

For (1), we have

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})=(a-b)+(c-\min(a,b,c)),
and similarly

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})=(b-c)+(\min(a,b,c)-a),
. Part (2) is also immediate. For (3) we have

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{2}})=\min(a,b)-c,\quad\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C,\gamma_{v_{2}})=a-\min(b,c).

By Lemma
4.32
we obtain

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C,\gamma_{v_{2}})-\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{2}})=a+c-\min(a,b)-\min(b,c)=\left[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(G,G_{v_{1}})\right]_{+},
as required. Finally, for Part (4), let us denote
m=\min(a,b,c),m^{\prime}=\min(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
. Then we have

\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}(C,C^{\prime})-\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,C^{\prime})=

\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
b&\min(b,c)&c\\
b^{\prime}&\min(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})&c^{\prime}\\
\end{matrix}\right|+\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
a&m&\min(b,c)\\
a^{\prime}&m^{\prime}&\min(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\\
\end{matrix}\right|-\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
a&\min(a,b)&b\\
a^{\prime}&\min(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})&b^{\prime}\\
\end{matrix}\right|-\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
\min(a,b)&m&c\\
\min(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})&m^{\prime}&c^{\prime}\\
\end{matrix}\right|=

(a+c)(b^{\prime}+m^{\prime})-(b+m)(a^{\prime}+c^{\prime})-

(\min(a,b)+\min(b,c))(b^{\prime}+m^{\prime}-a^{\prime}-c^{\prime})+(\min(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})+\min(b^{\prime},c^{\prime}))(b+m-a-c)=

(a+c-\min(a,b)-\min(b,c))(b^{\prime}+m^{\prime}-\min(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})-\min(b^{\prime},c^{\prime}))-

(b+m-\min(a,b)-\min(b,c))(a^{\prime}+c^{\prime}-\min(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})-\min(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})).

By Lemma
4.32
this equals

-[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})]_{+}[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C^{\prime},\gamma_{v_{1}})]_{-}+[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C,\gamma_{v_{1}})]_{-}[\sharp_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}(C^{\prime},\gamma_{v_{1}})]_{+}.
∎

Theorem 4.34
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}_{1},\mathfrak{W}_{2}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be two Demazure weaves, where the same braid word has been fixed for
\delta(\beta)
. Then the corresponding quivers
Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}
and
Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}
are related by a sequence of mutations.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.4
any two such Demazure weaves are related by a sequence of equivalence moves and weave mutations. By Corollary
4.29
and Lemma
4.30
equivalence moves for weaves do not change the quiver. By Lemma
4.33
and (
1
) a weave mutation corresponds to the quiver mutation in the cycle
\gamma_{v_{1}}
connecting two trivalent vertices.
∎

4.7.
Frozen vertices

Let
\mathfrak{W}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be a Demazure weave and let
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
be its associated quiver. Recall that the vertices of
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
are in bijection with the trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{W}
. In this section, we specify which vertices of
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
are frozen.

Definition 4.35
.

Let
v
be a trivalent vertex of
\mathfrak{W}
, equivalently a vertex of the quiver
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
, and
\gamma_{v}
its associated cycle. We say that
v
is
frozen
if there exists an edge
e\in E(\mathfrak{W})
on the southern boundary of
\mathfrak{W}
such that
\gamma_{v}(e)\neq 0
.

Definition
4.35
allows us to upgrade
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
to an ice quiver. Corollary
4.31
is refined as follows.

Lemma 4.36
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}_{1},\mathfrak{W}_{2}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be two equivalent weaves. Then the quivers
Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}
and
Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}
coincide as ice quivers, i.e. their frozen vertices coincide.

Proof.

For equivalences with only
4
- and
6
-valent vertices, let
v
be a frozen trivalent vertex and assume that the equivalence moves in the weave are performed after the appearance of the trivalent vertex
v
; otherwise the result is clear. Then, in the area where the moves are performed,
\gamma_{v}
is a Lusztig cycle and the result in this case now follows by Lemma
4.10
.
Now assume that
\mathfrak{W}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{W}_{2}
are related by a single equivalence involving a
3
-valent vertex, i.e. they are related by a move as in Example
4.12
. If
v
is not the trivalent vertex involved in the move, the computations in Example
4.12
imply the result. Else, the values of
\gamma_{v}
on the bottom of both weaves in Figure
4
are
(0,0,1)
and the result follows.
∎

The behavior that weave mutation has on these ice quivers is readily computed as well:

Lemma 4.37
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}_{1},\mathfrak{W}_{2}
be two weaves related by one mutation at a trivalent vertex
v\in Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}}
. Then:

(1)

The trivalent vertex
v
is not frozen.

(2)

The quivers
\mu_{v}(Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{1}})
and
Q_{\mathfrak{W}_{2}}
coincide as ice quivers.

Proof.

Part (1) is clear by the definition of weave mutation and Definition
4.35
. For Part (2) it suffices to notice that, if
C
is a cycle entering either one of the weaves in Figure
5
with weights
(a,b,c)
, the exiting weight is
\min(a,b,c)
, independently of the weave.
∎

4.8.
Quiver comparison for
\Delta\beta

In the study of braid varieties of the form
X(\Delta\beta)
, Lemma
3.16
established the isomorphism
X(\Delta\beta)\cong\Conf(\beta)
. Subsection
3.7
also described the quiver
Q(\beta)
, following
[
75
]
, which gives a cluster structure on the configuration space
\Conf(\beta)
. The purpose of the present subsection is to show that the quiver
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)}
for the right inductive weave
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)
, see Definitions
4.5
and
4.26
, coincides with the quiver
Q(\beta)
.

In Subsection
4.6
we assigned a sequence of roots
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{r}
, via Equation (
16
), to a horizontal slice of a weave spelling the word
\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{r}}
. By definition, in that case
\rho_{k}
is said to label the
k
-th strand of the weave. We now explain how strands labeled by simple roots are of particular relevance, starting with the following observation:

Lemma 4.38
.

(1)
The word
\beta
is reduced if and only if all roots
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{k}
are positive.

(2)
Let
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{r}}\in W
satisfy
\ell(w)=r
and assume that there exists a simple root
\alpha_{j}
,
j\in\mathsf{D}
, such that
w(-\alpha_{i_{r}})=\alpha_{j}
. Then
w
has a reduced expression starting with
s_{j}
.

Proof.

Part (1) is well known, see e.g
[
5
, Proposition 4.2.5]
. Let us prove Part (2). Since
s_{j}w(-\alpha_{i_{r}})=-\alpha_{j}
is a negative root, by (a) the word
s_{j}s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{r}}
is not reduced; since
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{r}}
is reduced the result follows.
∎

Lemma 4.39
.

Let
\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{r}}
be a horizontal slice of a weave which is reduced. Suppose that the
k
-th strand of this weave is labeled by a simple root
\alpha_{j}
. Then the following holds:

(1)

The
k
-th strand cannot enter a six-valent vertex through the middle.

(2)

If the
k
-th strand enters a six-valent vertex through the right
(
resp. left
)
then the
k-2
-nd
(
resp.
k+2
-nd
)
strand of the next horizontal slice is labeled by
\alpha_{j}
.

(3)

If the
k
-th strand enters a 4-valent vertex through the right
(
resp. left
)
then the
k-1
-st
(
resp.
k+1
-st
)
strand of the next horizontal slice is labeled by
\alpha_{j}
.

Proof.

The assumption states
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}(-\alpha_{i_{k}})=\alpha_{j}
, for each of the items we then have:

(1)

Assume that
i_{k-1}
is adjacent to
i_{k}
and
i_{k+1}=i_{k-1}
. Let
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-2}}
. Then
\alpha_{j}=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}(-\alpha_{i_{k}})=w(\alpha_{i_{k}}+\alpha_{i_{k-1}})
and it follows from Lemma
4.38
(a) that
ws_{i_{k-1}}
and
ws_{i_{k}}
cannot be simultaneously reduced. Since
ws_{i_{k-1}}
is reduced,
ws_{i_{k}}
is not and
ws_{i_{k-1}}s_{i_{k}}s_{i_{k+1}}=ws_{i_{k}}s_{i_{k-1}}s_{i_{k}}
is not reduced either. Contradiction.

(2)

This is a check based on
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}(-\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{j}
if
i
and
j
are adjacent.

(3)

This is also a check. ∎

Corollary 4.40
.

Let
\Delta=\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{r}}
be any reduced lift of
w_{0}
defining the positive roots
\rho_{k}
,
k=1,\dots,r
. For each
j\in\mathsf{D}
, consider

j_{0}:=\min\{1\leq k\leq r\mid\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{k}}\;\text{is not reduced}\}
and
\ j_{1}:=\min\{1\leq k\leq r\mid\rho_{k}=\alpha_{j}\}.

Then,
j_{0}=j_{1}
.

Proof.

Lemma
4.38
implies
j_{1}\geq j_{0}
. For
j_{1}\leq j_{0}
, Lemma
4.39
(b) and (c) imply that it is enough to find one reduced expression for
w_{0}
that satisfies this property. The expressions given in e.g.
[
2
, Table 1]
work, i.e. there exist such reduced expressions.
∎

Remark 4.41
.

We have defined the sequence of roots
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{r}
by reading
\beta
in a left-to-right fashion. We may read it in the opposite order to get a different sequence of roots
\rho^{\prime}_{r}=\alpha_{i_{r}}
,
\rho^{\prime}_{r-1}=s_{i_{r}}(\alpha_{i_{r-1}}),\rho^{\prime}_{r-2}=s_{i_{r}}s_{i_{r-1}}(\alpha_{i_{r-2}})\dots,\rho^{\prime}_{1}=s_{i_{r}}\cdots s_{i_{2}}(\alpha_{i_{1}})
. Alternatively,
\rho^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\rho^{\prime}_{r}
is the sequence
\rho
of roots for the opposite word
\text{\reflectbox{$\beta$}}:=\sigma_{i_{r}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{1}}
, but ordered oppositely:
\rho^{\prime}(\beta)_{i}=\rho(\text{\reflectbox{$\beta$}})_{r-i+1}
. Lemmas
4.38
,
4.39
and Corollary
4.40
are still valid with the appropriate modifications.

Let us now study the weaves of type
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)
inductively. Suppose that
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)
has been given, with its lower boundary being reduced expression for
\Delta
, that we also refer to as
\Delta
. By the right-handed version of Corollary
4.40
, the weave
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta\sigma_{i})
is obtained by taking the first strand (counting right to left) such that
\rho^{\prime}_{k}=\alpha_{i}
, and move this strand to the right in order to obtain a reduced word for
\Delta
that ends in
\sigma_{i}
. By Lemma
4.39
, in the process of doing this the strand will
not
enter a
6
-valent vertex from the middle so, if there was a cycle containing this strand, it will not bifurcate. Lemma
4.39
also implies that any cycle containing a strand labeled by a simple root will not bifurcate. Once we have finished moving the strand to the right, we pair it with the strand coming from the rightmost
\sigma_{i}
. This ends the cycle containing the strand that has been moved (if any) and creates a new cycle starting at the new trivalent vertex. Note that this new cycle is labeled by the positive root
\alpha_{i}
. This discussion implies the following:

Lemma 4.42
.

Every cycle in the inductive weave
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)
is non-bifurcating, and all of its weights are equal to 0 or 1.

For finer information, we first fix some notation. For every enumeration
\kappa
of vertices of the Dynkin diagram
\mathsf{D}
, we have a reduced expression
\Delta(\kappa)=\Delta_{n}^{(\kappa)}\cdots\Delta_{1}^{(\kappa)}
of the half-twist
\Delta
, so that
\Delta_{m}^{(\kappa)}\cdots\Delta_{1}^{(\kappa)}
is a reduced expression of the longest element of the Weyl group of the Dynkin diagram consisting of the first
m
vertices (under the enumeration
\kappa
) of
\mathsf{D}
. Let us
fix
an enumeration of
\mathsf{D}
, and we denote
\Delta=\Delta_{n}\cdots\Delta_{1}
the reduced decomposition of
\Delta
corresponding to this fixed enumeration. Note that this implies that the first strand (reading from right to left) that is labeled by
\alpha_{i}
(as in Remark
4.41
) is the leftmost strand on
\Delta_{i}
. Note also that every other enumeration corresponds to an element in the symmetric group
S_{n}
. For
i=1,\dots,n
, let us denote by
\Delta(i)
a reduced expression of
\Delta
corresponding to the enumeration given by the permutation
(12\cdots i)
, that is, corresponding to the enumeration
(i,1,\dots,i-1,i+1,\dots,n)
of the vertices of
\mathsf{D}
. Note that the rightmost strand of
\Delta(i)
has color
i
and is labeled by
\sigma_{i}
.

In order to obtain the inductive weave
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)
, we iteratively build the weaves
\mathfrak{w}_{0}:=\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta)
,
\mathfrak{w}_{1}:=\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\sigma_{i_{1}})
,
\mathfrak{w}_{2}:=\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}),\cdots,\mathfrak{w}_{r}:=\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)
. In fact, we build these weaves as follows:

-

The bottom boundary of the weave
\mathfrak{w}_{k}
is
\Delta=\Delta_{n}\cdots\Delta_{1}
for every
k=0,\dots,r
.

-

To build
\mathfrak{w}_{k+1}
from
\mathfrak{w}_{k}
, we use braid moves to change the bottom boundary of
\mathfrak{w}_{k}
to
\Delta(i_{k+1})
.
The rightmost strand of
\Delta(i_{k+1})
is labeled by
\sigma_{i_{k+1}}
, and we may form a new trivalent vertex in
\mathfrak{w}_{k+1}
. After, we use braid moves to return the bottom boundary to
\Delta
.

Definition 4.43
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a weave and
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
its corresponding quiver. For
i\in\mathsf{D}
, a vertex of
Q_{\mathfrak{W}}
is said to be an
i
-vertex if it corresponds to an
i
-colored trivalent vertex of
\mathfrak{W}
.
(
Compare with Section
3.7
.
)

By Lemma
4.42
, the quiver
Q_{\mathfrak{w}_{k}}
has a frozen
i
-vertex if and only if there exists a (necessarily unique) cycle that has a nonzero weight on the leftmost strand of
\Delta_{i}
in the bottom boundary. In particular,
Q_{\mathfrak{w}_{k}}
has at most one frozen
i
-vertex for every
i\in\mathsf{D}
.

Proposition 4.44
.

Let
i\in\mathsf{D}
and let
f(i)\in Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)}
be the unique
(
if any
)
frozen
i
-vertex. Then, the quiver
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta\sigma_{i})}
is obtained from
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)}
by the following procedure.

(1)

Thaw the vertex
f(i)
and add a new frozen vertex
f^{\prime}(i)
, together with an arrow
f(i)\to f^{\prime}(i)
.

(2)

If
j
is adjacent to
i
in
\mathsf{D}
and the vertex
f(j)
was added after
f(i)
, add an arrow
f(j)\to f(i)
.

(3)

If
i
is adjacent to
j
in
\mathsf{D}
, add an arrow of weight
1/2
from the frozen vertex
f^{\prime}(i)
to the frozen vertex
f(j)
.

Proof.

To obtain the weave
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta\sigma_{i})
from
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)
, we have to take the left-most strand of
\Delta_{i}
and move it to the right. The vertex
f(i)
exists if and only if this strand is carrying a cycle, that we call
C(i)
. By Lemma
4.42
, the cycle will end at the new trivalent vertex in
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta\sigma_{i})
, which corresponds to
f^{\prime}(i)
. Thus, Part (1) is clear. For Part (2), we use Lemma
4.28
to count the new intersections that are formed in
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta\sigma_{i})
. We only look at the portion of the weave that is between the bottom boundary of
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)
(corresponding to the braid word
\Delta
) and the new trivalent vertex in
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta\sigma_{i})
(so that the bottom boundary is
\Delta(i)
).
By Lemma
4.39
, the top and bottom boundaries of the cycle
C(j)
consist of a single strand labeled by
\alpha_{j}
. The permutation
p_{i}=(12\cdots i)
satisfies the property that if
a<b
but
p_{i}(b)<p_{i}(a)
, then
b=i
. Thus, the only new intersections involve the cycle
C(i)
, and these intersections may only involve cycles
C(j)
where
j
is adjacent to
i
in
\mathsf{D}
and
j<i
. Now we compute

(17)

\sharp_{\mathrm{top}}C(i)\cdot C(j)-\sharp_{\mathrm{bottom}}C(i)\cdot C(j)=\begin{cases}-1&j<i,\,\text{and}\,(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})\neq 0\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Let us now look at the intersections that are formed after the trivalent vertex. These intersections will only involve
C^{\prime}(i)
, where
C^{\prime}(i)
is the cycle that has started at this trivalent vertex. Similarly, we have

(18)

\sharp_{\mathrm{top}}C^{\prime}(i)\cdot C(j)-\sharp_{\mathrm{bottom}}C^{\prime}(i)\cdot C(j)=\begin{cases}+1&j<i,\,\text{and}\,(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})\neq 0\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Now, if
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})\neq 0
and
f(j)
was added after
f(i)
, then we observe an arrow
f(j)\to f(i)
from (
17
) if
j<i
and from (
18
) if
i<j
. If
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})\neq 0
and
f(j)
was added before
f(i)
, then either we do not observe any intersections (if
i<j
) or the terms (
17
) and (
18
) cancel. Finally, we need to study the (half-weighted) arrows between
f^{\prime}(i)
and
f(j)
for
j\neq i
. It follows easily from (
18
) and the fact that
f(j)
is on a strand with root
\alpha_{j}
that (3) above holds. The result follows.
∎

Inductively, the analysis above concludes the following result.

Corollary 4.45
.

Let
\beta\in\Br_{W}^{+}
be a braid word and
Q(\beta)
the quiver for the initial seed for
\Conf(\beta)
, as introduced in Section
3.7
. Then
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\Delta\beta)}=Q(\beta)
.

4.9.
Quivers for inductive weaves

The inductive weaves
\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta),\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)
in Definition
4.5
depend on the braid
word
for
\beta
and not only on the braid
\beta
. In this section, we examine the dependency of the quivers
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
and
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
on the choice of braid word. First, we have the following result.

Proposition 4.46
.

Let
i,j\in\mathsf{D}
be adjacent vertices in the Dynkin diagram
\mathsf{D}
. Consider the two braid words
\beta=\beta_{2}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\beta_{1}
and
\beta^{\prime}=\beta_{2}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\beta_{1}
, which differ by a single braid move. Then the following holds:

(1)

The quivers
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
and
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta^{\prime})}
are identical if
\delta(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\beta_{1})\neq\delta(\beta_{1})
. Similarly,
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
and
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta^{\prime})}
are identical if
\delta(\beta_{2}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i})\neq\delta(\beta_{2})
.

(2)

Else, the quivers
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
and
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta^{\prime})}

(
resp.
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
and
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta^{\prime})})
are related by a single mutation at the vertex given by the middle letter in the braid move.

Proof.

Let us focus on right inductive weaves, as the proof for the left inductive weave
\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}
is analogous. The statement (2) follows by studying the two right inductive weaves in Figures
9
and
10
, which correspond to those for
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
respectively. In these figures, the cycles are indicated with colors, as depicted on the right, and the quivers are related by a mutation at the green vertex.

The proof of (1) is similar. The key observation is that any two weaves starting from the same braid word in the list

\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j};\ \sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i}\sim\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i};\ \sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i};\ \sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sim\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}

and ending at
\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}
are equivalent.
∎

Figure 9.
(Left) Right inductive weave for
\beta_{2}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}
. (Center) The intersection quiver associated to some of the Lusztig cycles. (Right) Some of the Lusztig cycles depicted in the weave. Their colors match the colors of the corresponding vertices in the quiver.

Figure 10.
(Left) The right inductive weave for
\beta_{2}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}
. (Center) The intersection quiver for some of its Lusztig cycles. The quiver here is obtained from that in Figure
9
by mutating at the green vertex. Note that the arrow from the blue vertex to the purple vertex appears only if these cycles are not frozen in the right inductive weave of
\beta
. (Right) Some of the Lusztig cycles in the weave.

Finally, we can establish the relation between the quivers
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
and
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)}
. It reads as follows:

Lemma 4.47
.

Let
\beta
be a braid word and consider the possible two cases:
\delta(\sigma_{i}\beta)=\delta(\beta)
or
\delta(\sigma_{i}\beta)\neq\delta(\beta)
.

If
\delta(\sigma_{i}\beta)=\delta(\beta)
, let
v\in\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)
be the last trivalent vertex of the weave
\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)
. Then:

(1)

The vertex
v
is frozen and it is a source in
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)}
.

(2)

The quiver
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
can be obtained from
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)}
by the following procedure:

–

Remove the frozen vertex
v
.

–

Freeze all vertices that were incident with
v
.

–

Remove possible arrows between frozen vertices.

If else
\delta(\sigma_{i}\beta)=s_{i}\delta(\beta)
, then the quivers
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)}
and
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
coincide.

Proof.

First we consider the case
\delta(\sigma_{i}\beta)=\delta(\beta)
. By Definition
4.5
, the left inductive weave
\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)
is obtained from the weave
\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)
by adding a new
i
-colored trivalent vertex
v
. The vertex in the quiver associated to the Lusztig cycle for this trivalent vertex
v
is frozen, because of Definition
4.35
and the fact that the Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{v}
flows straight down to the southern boundary of
\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)
. Independently, the (upper) left arm of the trivalent vertex
v
goes all the way to the top of
\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)
and thus
v
is a source in the quiver
Q_{\overleftarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\sigma_{i}\beta)}
. (See for example Definition
4.17
or cf. Figure
32
in Subsection
11.1
.) This directly establishes
(1)
and, by construction, also
(2)
. Second, in the case that
\delta(\sigma_{i}\beta)=s_{i}\delta(\beta)
, there are no trivalent vertices added because of Definition
4.5
. Therefore the quivers are identical in this case.
∎

Remark 4.48
.

The appropriate modification of Lemma
4.47
is valid for the right inductive weaves
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}
. The quiver
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta)}
is obtained from
Q_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}(\beta\sigma_{i})}
by removing a frozen sink, provided that
\delta(\beta)=\delta(\beta\sigma_{i})
.

5.
Construction of cluster structures

In this section we focus on simply laced cases. We introduce the (to be) cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables, which will be indexed by trivalent vertices of a Demazure weave, study their properties and prove Theorem
1.1
.

5.1.
Framed weaves and framed flags

Given a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}
, the cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables associated to
\mathfrak{W}
will be extracted from the information of framed flags compatible with
\mathfrak{W}
. Intuitively, this translates to studying all possible assignments of a framed flag to every connected component of the complement of
\mathfrak{W}
satisfying certain incidence conditions dictated by
\mathfrak{W}
. See
[
20
, Section 5]
or
[
19
, Section 4]
for the origin of such ideas, related to the microlocal theory of sheaves.

In order to make the cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables computable, we introduce appropriate coordinates. This is done in a manner that we effectively assign an element

g\in\mathsf{G}
to each component of the complement of
\mathfrak{W}
, not just a flag. These elements
g\in\mathsf{G}
indeed parametrize flags, so the associated flags
g\mathsf{U}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}
and
g\mathsf{B}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
are the main geometric objects, but the elements themselves are useful in our construction and when performing computations. We now introduce the notions of a raked weave and a labeling, following
[
17
, Section 4]
, which allow us to describe such matters with precision.

5.1.1.
Raked weaves

Consider a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}\subset R
for a positive braid word
\beta
. Following Subsection
4.1
,
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
denotes a fixed rectangle and the weave
\mathfrak{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is considered inside of
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
in such a way that
\mathfrak{W}\cap\partial R
only has points in the northern and southern edges of
\partial R
. The boundary
\partial R
of the rectangle
R
is piecewise linear: we refer to its four linear components
\partial_{\mathsf{n}}R,\partial_{\mathsf{s}}R,\partial_{\mathsf{e}}R
and
\partial_{\mathsf{w}}R
as the north, south, east and west boundaries, respectively. Here
\partial_{\mathsf{n}}R
and
\partial_{\mathsf{s}}R
, resp.
\partial_{\mathsf{e}}R
and
\partial_{\mathsf{w}}R
, are parallel.

By definition, a horizontal slice of
\mathfrak{W}
is any segment in
R
parallel to
\partial_{\mathsf{n}}R
that starts at
\partial_{\mathsf{w}}R
and ends at
\partial_{\mathsf{e}}R
. We henceforth assume that Demazure weaves
\mathfrak{W}\subset R
have the property that any horizontal slice contains at most one vertex
v\in V(\mathfrak{W})
, i.e. no two different vertices in
\mathfrak{W}
are at the same horizontal height. Therefore, any vertex
v\in V(\mathfrak{W})
uniquely defines a horizontal slice
H_{v}\subset R
by requiring
v\in H_{v}
. Following
[
17
, Section 4.1]
, we use certain decorations added to the weave, as follows.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}\subset R
be a Demazure weave. For each trivalent vertex
v\in V(\mathfrak{W})
, consider the horizontal segment
r_{v}\subset H_{v}
that starts at
v
and ends at
\partial_{\mathsf{e}}R
. By definition, the raked weave
\mathfrak{W}^{=}\subset R
associated to
\mathfrak{W}
is the planar graph given by

\mathfrak{W}^{=}:=\mathfrak{W}\cup\left(\bigcup_{v\in V(\mathfrak{W})}r_{v}\right),

where vertices
V(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
and edges
E(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
are defined as follows:

(1)

Every vertex
v\in V(\mathfrak{W})
is a vertex
v\in V(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
, i.e.
V(\mathfrak{W})\subset V(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
, and the remaining vertices
V(\mathfrak{W}^{=})\setminus V(\mathfrak{W})
are in bijection with the collection of intersection points of the form
(r_{v}\cap\mathfrak{W})\setminus\{v\}
, where
v\in V(\mathfrak{W})
is a trivalent vertex.

(2)

An edge

e\in E(\mathfrak{W}^{=})

exists between a pair of vertices

v,v^{\prime}\in V(\mathfrak{W}^{=})

if and only if there exists a connected component of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}\setminus V(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
whose closure contains
v
and
v^{\prime}
.
3
3

3

In particular, every edge
e\in E(\mathfrak{W})
that does not intersect any
r_{v}
,
v\in V(\mathfrak{W})
, defines a unique edge of
E(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
. Similarly, if an edge
e\in E(\mathfrak{W})
intersects a collection of rays
r_{v_{1}},\ldots r_{v_{q}}
, for some trivalent vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{q}\in V(\mathfrak{W})
, then there is a unique edge between the vertices defined by
r_{v_{i}}\cap e
and
r_{v_{i+1}}\cap e
. Finally, if a ray
r_{v}
intersects a collection of edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}
, for some
e_{1},\ldots,e_{q}\in E(\mathfrak{W})
, then there is a unique edge between
r_{v}\cap e_{i}
and
r_{v}\cap e_{i+1}
.

The vertices in
V(\mathfrak{W}^{=})\setminus V(\mathfrak{W})
are referred to as virtual vertices. An edge in
E(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
which is not contained in any edge of
\mathfrak{W}
is referred to as a dashed edge; a non-dashed edge is said to be solid.

Figure 11.
A Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}
(left) and its associated raked weave
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
(right). The
virtual vertices have been marked with green dots, for clarity. The raking rays
r_{v}
emanating from trivalent vertices
v
of
\mathfrak{W}
are always drawn with dashed yellow lines.

Figure
11
depicts the raked weave associated to the weave in Figure
7
from Example
4.6
.(ii). Definition
5.1
is used in Definition
5.8
below, which introduces a key notion in our construction. To simplify notation, we denote the subset of dashed edges in
E(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
by
E_{dh}(\mathfrak{W})
and its complement by
E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
, the subscript abbreviating
solid
. In figures, we draw the raking rays
r_{v}
by
dashed yellow
lines, as in
[
17
, Section 4]
.

Remark 5.2
.

Note that
E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
contains more edges than
E(\mathfrak{W})
. The edges in
E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
are in bijection with the edges of
\mathfrak{W}^{\circ}
, where
\mathfrak{W}^{\circ}
is the graph obtained from
\mathfrak{W}
by adding one bivalent vertex per each intersection point of the form
r_{v}\cap e
,
e\in E(\mathfrak{W})
.

5.1.2.
Labeled weaves

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a Demazure weave. In order to construct the cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables, we will label the solid edges of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
by pairs of
\mathbb{C}
-valued rational functions on
X(\beta)
and the dashed edges by
\mathsf{U}
-valued rational functions on
X(\beta)
. For each solid edge
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
, we denote such pair of rational functions by
\widetilde{z}_{e},u_{e}:X(\beta)\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and typically write
(\widetilde{z}_{e},u_{e})
to the right of the weave edge to indicate this assignment, e.g. see Figure
12
. These
\widetilde{z}_{e}
and
u_{e}
are referred to as the
\widetilde{z}
and
u
-variables of the edge
e
. This labeling is a framed enhancement of the labeling defined in Section
4.1
, see e.g. Figure
2
.

Given a rational function
f:X(\beta)\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we denote its (maximal) domain of definition by
\mathfrak{D}(f)\subset X(\beta)
.

Definition 5.3
.

A labeled weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is a pair consisting of a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}
for a positive braid word
\beta
together with a pair of functions
\zeta=(\zeta_{sd},\zeta_{dh})
such that

(i)

\zeta_{sd}:E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}(X(\beta))\times\mathbb{C}(X(\beta))
assigns two rational functions
\zeta(e):=(\widetilde{z}_{e},u_{e})
to each solid edge
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
.

(ii)

\zeta_{dh}:E_{dh}(\mathfrak{W})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}(X(\beta),\mathsf{U})
assigns a
\mathsf{U}
-valued rational function on
X(\beta)
to each dashed edge
e\in E_{dh}(\mathfrak{W})
.

Given a labeled weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
, we denote by

\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta):=\left(\bigcap_{e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})}(\mathfrak{D}(\widetilde{z}_{e})\cap\mathfrak{D}(u_{e})\cap\mathfrak{D}(u_{e}^{-1}))\right)\cap\left(\bigcap_{e\in E_{dh}(\mathfrak{W})}\mathfrak{D}(\zeta_{dh}(e))\right)

the domain of definition of the labeling
\zeta
. That is,
\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is the the maximal open subset of
X(\beta)
where all the rational functions
\widetilde{z}_{e}
,
u_{e}
and
\zeta_{dh}(e)
in the labels are defined, and
u_{e}
are invertible.

Given a labeled weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
as in Definition
5.3
, any point
p\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
specifies another labeled weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta_{p})
whose labels are the constant rational functions
\zeta_{p}(e):=(z_{e}(p),u_{e}(p))
, if
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
, or
\zeta_{p}(e):=\zeta_{dh}(e)(p)
if
e\in E_{dh}(\mathfrak{W})
.

Figure 12.
Framed flags near a labeled solid edge
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
of a weave
\mathfrak{W}
.

The purpose of the labels in Definition
5.3
is to record information about framed flags, using the coordinates introduced in Subsections
3.4
and
3.5
.
We recall that we parameterize framed flags as
x\mathsf{U}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}
, where
x\in\mathsf{G}
and
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathsf{G}
is a fixed unipotent subgroup, cf. Subsection
3.5
. Also, given a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}\subset R
, we refer to the connected components of
R\setminus\mathfrak{W}
as regions, and similarly for the associated raked weave
\mathfrak{W}^{=}\subset R
. A region of
R\setminus\mathfrak{W}^{=}
whose closure intersects the east boundary
\partial_{\mathsf{e}}R
is said to be a rightmost region. There are as many rightmost regions as there are trivalent vertices in
\mathfrak{W}
plus one. Note that there is a unique well-defined leftmost region, whose closure intersects the west boundary
\partial_{\mathsf{w}}R
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
be a labeled weave and
p\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
a point in its domain. By definition, a collection of framed flags indexed by the regions of
R\setminus\mathfrak{W}^{=}
is said to be compatible with the labeled weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta_{p})
, if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

The framed flag indexed by the leftmost region is the standard framed flag
\mathsf{U}
, and the framed flag indexed by any of the rightmost regions projects to
\delta(\beta)\mathsf{B}
in
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
. That is, the flag underlying the rightmost framed flag is
\delta(\beta)\mathsf{B}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
.

(2)

Given a solid edge
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
of color
i\in\mathsf{D}
, we denote by
F_{\mathsf{w}}
and
F_{\mathsf{e}}
the two framed flags respectively west and east of
e
, i.e.
F_{\mathsf{w}}
is to the left of
e
and
F_{\mathsf{e}}
is to the right of
e
. We impose the condition that there exists
x\in\mathsf{G}
such that
F_{\mathsf{w}}=x\mathsf{U}
and

(19)

F_{\mathsf{e}}=xB_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{e}(p))\chi_{i}(u_{e}(p))\mathsf{U}.

That is, the framed flag
F_{\mathsf{e}}
to the right of
e
is obtained, as dictated by this formula, from the framed flag
F_{\mathsf{w}}
to the left of
e
through the information in the label
\zeta_{p}(e)
.

(3)

Given a dashed edge
e\in E_{dh}(\mathfrak{W})
, we denote by
F_{\mathsf{n}}
and
F_{\mathsf{s}}
the two framed flags respectively north and south of
e
. Then we require
F_{\mathsf{n}}=F_{\mathsf{s}}
.

By definition, the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
of framed flags associated to
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is the space of collections of framed flags compatible with
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta_{p})
for some
p\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
.

Note that
\mathfrak{M}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
in Definition
5.4
is naturally an algebraic variety. Indeed, it is a Zariski closed subset of the algebraic variety
\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)\times(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U})^{r}
, where
r=|\pi_{0}(R\setminus\mathfrak{W})|
is the number of regions of
R\setminus\mathfrak{W}
. In the unframed case and
\mathsf{G}=\SL_{m}
, these assignments of flags for each region in
R\setminus\mathfrak{W}
, with transversality conditions as imposed by
\mathfrak{W}
, led to the flag moduli space introduced and studied in
[
20
, Section 5]
, cf. also Section
4
above.
4
4

4

In that case, one can work rather directly with
\mathfrak{W}
, without using its raked refinement
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
.

Remark 5.5
.

For convenience, we label the edges in local models near vertices of the weave according to their (inter)cardinal directions, as depicted in Figure
13
, e.g.
e_{\mathsf{n}\mathsf{w}}
stands for the edge pointing northwest.
5
5

5

In particular,
e
can denote either an edge or the East direction, but the meaning is always clear from the context and we use different fonts (
e
for edges and
\mathsf{e}
for East).
To ease notation, we also write
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{nw}})=(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}},u_{\mathsf{nw}})
instead of
\zeta_{sd}(e_{\mathsf{nw}})=(\widetilde{z}_{e_{\mathsf{nw}}},u_{e_{\mathsf{nw}}})
, and similarly for all the other directions and for dashed edges. In particular, we suppress the subscript of the labeling
\zeta(e)
if it is clear by context if it is applied to a solid edge, and thus
\zeta(e)=\zeta_{sd}(e)
, or to a dashed edge, where the notation would be read as
\zeta(e)=\zeta_{dh}(e)
.

Figure 13.
Cardinal notation for edges near the types of vertices in
\mathfrak{W}
. From left to right: a trivalent vertex
v
, with the dashed ray
r_{v}
in yellow, a hexavalent vertex, a tetravalent vertex and a virtual vertex. Technically, virtual vertices are tetravalent, but we reserve that notation for the vertices in the third column, strictly coming from intersections of (solid) weave edges. Similarly, trivalent vertices
v\in V(\mathfrak{W})
become tetravalent in
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
, but we still refer to them as trivalent.

Remark 5.6
.

The data of a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}
alone is enough to describe a moduli space of compatible framed flags as in Definition
5.4
, without using
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
. That said, adding the raking rays
r_{v}
, which is the additional information contained in
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
, essentially allows us to assign elements of
\mathsf{G}
in every region. This data of an element
g\in\mathsf{G}
in every region, and not just its framed flag coset
g\mathsf{U}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}
, has the advantage of simplifying certain computations in our arguments. This choice of an element
g\in\mathsf{G}
for each region of
R\setminus\mathfrak{W}^{=}
would not be well-defined if we did not refine
\mathfrak{W}
to
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
, only their framed flag cosets would be well-defined.

Now, the intuition is that we want
\mathfrak{M}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
in Definition
5.4
to be isomorphic to a torus, which will underlie a cluster torus in
X(\beta)
. Nevertheless, an arbitrary labeling
\zeta
of a weave
\mathfrak{W}
for
\beta
is not sufficient: we must add conditions to a labeling
\zeta
for that to hold. We also need the following piece of notation:

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a Dynkin diagram. For each vertex
i\in\mathsf{D}
, we denote by
\xi_{i}:\mathsf{U}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{C},+)
the unique additive character on
\mathsf{U}
such that

\xi_{i}\left[\varphi_{j}\left(\begin{matrix}1&a\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\right]=\delta_{ij}a.

5.1.3.
Framed weaves

Let us impose additional conditions on a labeled weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
so as to proceed with our construction of cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables, as follows.

Definition 5.8
.

A labeled weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is said to be a framed weave if
\zeta
satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

If
e_{i}\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
is the solid weave edge corresponding to the
i
th crossing of
\beta
, starting at the (top) northern boundary of
\mathfrak{W}
, then we require the condition

(\widetilde{z}_{e_{i}},u_{e_{i}})=(z_{i},1),

where
z_{i}:X(\beta)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the regular function introduced in Subsection
3.4
.

(2)

In a trivalent vertex of
\mathfrak{W}
, using the notation in Figure
13
(left), we require

\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}}=\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}-u_{\mathsf{nw}}^{-2}\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad u_{\mathsf{s}}=\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{ne}}.

In additional, the unique dashed edge
e_{v}
starting at this vertex is labeled by

\zeta_{dh}(e_{v})=\varphi_{i}\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1}u_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-2}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathsf{U}.

(3)

In a hexavalent vertex of
\mathfrak{W}
, using the notation in Figure
13
(center), we require

\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{sw}}u_{\mathsf{n}}=\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}},\qquad\widetilde{z}_{s}u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{s}}=\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}}^{2}u_{\mathsf{ne}}-\widetilde{z}_{n}u_{\mathsf{sw}}u_{\mathsf{s}},\qquad\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{se}}u_{\mathsf{sw}}=\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{s}},

\qquad u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{n}}=u_{\mathsf{s}}u_{\mathsf{se}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad u_{\mathsf{n}}u_{\mathsf{ne}}=u_{\mathsf{sw}}u_{\mathsf{s}}.

(4)

In a tetravalent vertex of
\mathfrak{W}
, using the notation in Figure
13
(right), we require

\zeta(e_{\mathsf{nw}})=\zeta(e_{\mathsf{se}})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\zeta(e_{\mathsf{ne}})=\zeta(e_{\mathsf{sw}}).

(5)

In a virtual vertex we write
Y_{\mathsf{w}}:=\zeta_{dh}(e_{\mathsf{w}})
and
Y_{\mathsf{e}}:=\zeta_{dh}(e_{\mathsf{e}})
, where
e_{\mathsf{w}},e_{\mathsf{e}}
are the dashed edges west and east of the virtual vertex. Suppose that the solid edge
e_{\mathsf{n}}
north of the virtual vertex has color
i\in\mathsf{D}
. Then we require

\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}}=\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{n}}+\xi_{i}(Y_{\mathsf{w}}),\quad u_{\mathsf{s}}=u_{\mathsf{n}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad Y_{\mathsf{e}}=(B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathbf{s}}))^{-1}\cdot Y_{\mathsf{w}}\cdot(B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{n}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{n}})).

Figure 14.
(Left) Example of framed weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
with the weave
\mathfrak{W}
being a trivalent vertex, considered at the top of
\mathfrak{W}
so that
u_{1}=u_{2}=1
and
\widetilde{z}_{1}=z_{1}
,
\widetilde{z}_{2}=z_{2}
. (Right) A general framed weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
with
\mathfrak{W}
a trivalent vertex, possibly inside of a weave
\mathfrak{W}
. The dashed yellow lines have not been depicted to improve visual ease.

The conditions in Definition
5.8
are designed to describe framed flags compatible with
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
in a manner that
\mathfrak{M}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is (isomorphic to) the initial cluster torus in
X(\beta)
associated to the Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}
and cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables can be read directly from
\zeta
. This will be apparent in the proof of Theorem
5.12
. In particular, Condition (1) in Definition
5.8
captures the equation in Corollary
3.7
. Conditions (2), (3), (4) and (5) are consistency conditions for such collection of framed flags to exist, as explained by the following result.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
be a framed weave. Then the following holds:

For any
p\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
, there exists a unique collection of framed flags compatible with
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta_{p})
. In particular, the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
.

For any (solid) weave edge
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
at the southern boundary of
\mathfrak{W}
, we have
\widetilde{z}_{e}=0
.

Proof.

For Part (a), start with the standard framed flag
\mathsf{U}
in the leftmost (westmost) region. For any other region, choose a path connecting that region with the leftmost region that avoids all vertices of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
and is transverse to the edges of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
. Then assign framed flags to regions along the path using condition (
19
) in Definition
5.4
. If we choose a horizontal path near the top of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
(which coincides with the top of
\mathfrak{W}
), parallel to
\partial_{\mathsf{n}}R
, Condition (1) in Definition
5.8
and the definition of
X(\beta)
ensure that the framed flag assigned to the (top) rightmost region of that path has underlying flag
\delta(\beta)\mathsf{B}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
. Condition (3) in Definition
5.4
then implies that the framed flag assigned to any rightmost region in
R\setminus\mathfrak{W}^{=}
also projects to
\delta(\beta)\mathsf{B}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
.

It remains to verify that this assignment of flags is independent of the choice of paths. This can be checked locally near each vertex: comparing the condition imposed by a path above and below the vertex. For a trivalent vertex, Condition (2) captures the framed version of identity (
11
), which reads:

(20)

B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{nw}})B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{ne}})=B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}})\chi_{i}\left(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{ne}}\right)\varphi_{i}\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1}u_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-2}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right).

Thus, the assignment of flags is independent of whether we cross
e_{\mathsf{nw}}
and
e_{\mathsf{ne}}
or
e_{\mathsf{s}}
. Similarly, for a hexavalent vertex, Condition (3) captures the property discussed in Lemma
3.12
(2), which implies independence of the path. Condition (4) captures the usual fact that nothing particularly interesting happens at a tetravalent vertex. Finally, for a virtual vertex, Condition (5) is equivalent to

Y_{\mathsf{w}}B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{n}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{n}})=B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{s}})Y_{\mathsf{e}}

and Lemma
4.2
implies that
Y_{\mathsf{e}}\in\mathsf{U}
, see (
12
). In summary, conditions
(2)
,
(3)
,
(4)
and
(5)
in Definition
5.8
imply independence of the chosen path.

For Part
(b)
, choose a horizontal path near the bottom (southern boundary) of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
, parallel to
\partial_{\mathbf{s}}R
, where the edges spell a reduced expression for the Demazure product
\delta(\beta)
. Lemma
3.2
then directly implies that
\widetilde{z}_{e}=0
for all solid edges
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
at the bottom of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
, i.e. for those edges intersecting
\partial_{\mathbf{s}}R
. (Alternatively, this also follows from identity (
8
).)
∎

5.1.4.
The
\widetilde{z}
-variables as Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables

The following algebraic property will be a key step in the construction of cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables in Theorem
5.12
. Intuitively, it states that given
u
-variables satisfying the conditions of a labeled weave, there is a unique way to find
\widetilde{z}
-variables such that they are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables and they all together form a framed weave.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a weave. Consider a collection of variables
\underline{u}=\{u_{e}\}_{e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})}
satisfying Condition
(1)
, the two equations on the bottom line of Condition
(3)
, and the equations in the
u
-variables in Conditions
(4)
and
(5)
, i.e. the identities in the
u
-arguments of a labeling.
6
6

6

All these are conditions from Definition
5.8
. Also, in this lemma we do not require that the
u
-variables
\underline{u}
are rational functions in the initial top variables
\{z_{i}\}
.

Then there exists a unique collection of Laurent polynomials
\{\widetilde{z}_{e}(\underline{u})\}_{e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})}
and
\{Y_{e}(\underline{u})\}_{e\in E_{dh}(\mathfrak{W})}
such that
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is a framed weave, where
\zeta
is the labeling given by

\zeta_{sd}(e):=(\widetilde{z}_{e}(\underline{u}),u_{e})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\zeta_{dh}(e):=Y_{e}(u).

In particular, the variables
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell(\beta)]}
labeling the top edges of the weave
\mathfrak{W}
are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables
\underline{u}
. In addition, the variables
\{z_{i}\}
satisfy the defining equations of
X(\beta)
.

Proof.

We prove the statement by scanning the weave
\mathfrak{W}
from bottom to top. At the bottom (southern) boundary of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
, near
\partial_{\mathsf{s}}R
, we declare all
\widetilde{z}
-variables to be zero and assign framed flags to the corresponding regions as in the proof of Lemma
5.9
.(a). In particular, this implies that the framed flag assigned to that bottom rightmost (eastmost) region must have underlying flag
\delta(\beta)\mathsf{B}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
.
7
7

7

Definition
5.4
.(3) then implies that all rightmost regions will be assigned the same
framed flag that projects to
\delta(\beta)\mathsf{B}
.
Now we start scanning up the weave
\mathfrak{W}
, bottom to top by horizontal slices, and argue inductively. The base case is the bottom boundary, just discussed in this paragraph. Let us proceed with the inductive step:

(1)

If we cross a trivalent vertex, the inductive assumption is that
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}}
is a Laurent polynomial in the
u
-variables, where
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}}
is the
z
-variable of the edge
e_{\mathsf{s}}
at a trivalent vertex. Condition (2) implies that
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}
is a Laurent monomial in
\underline{u}
, while
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}
is a linear combination of
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}}
and another Laurent monomial in
\underline{u}
. Therefore, both
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}
and
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}
are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables.

(2)

Now, when crossing the trivalent vertex
v
in item
(1)
above, there are
\widetilde{z}
-variables to the right of
v
that also change when crossing the dashed edges inside the raking ray
r_{v}
associated to
v
. First, by Definition
5.8
.(2), the label
\zeta_{dh}(e_{v})=Y_{e_{v}}
for the dashed edge
e_{v}\in E_{dh}(\mathfrak{W})
contained in
r_{v}
and intersecting
v
is

(21)

\zeta_{dh}(e_{v})=\varphi_{i}\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1}u_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-2}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathsf{U}.

By item
(1)
above, the label
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}
is a Laurent polynomial in the
u
-variables. Therefore the components of
\zeta_{dh}(e_{v})
are indeed Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables, i.e.
\zeta_{dh}(e_{v})
defines a regular morphism
\Spec\mathbb{C}[\underline{u}^{\pm 1}]\to\mathsf{U}
.
8
8

8

It is a matrix with Laurent polynomial entries in the
u
-variables if
\mathsf{G}
is a matrix group.

Let
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{q}\in V(\mathfrak{W}^{=})
be the vertices in
r_{v}
, starting with the trivalent vertex
v_{0}=v
and continuing with the virtual vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{q}
to the right of
v
, ordered left to right. Let
e_{v}^{i}
be the dashed edge between
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
, so that
e_{v}^{0}=e_{v}
. Denote by
\widetilde{z}^{(i)}_{\mathsf{n}}
and
\widetilde{z}^{(i)}_{\mathsf{s}}
the
\widetilde{z}
-variables north and south of the vertex
v_{i}
,
i\in[q]
, and similarly for the corresponding
u
-variables. We now argue by induction on
i
that
\zeta_{dh}(e^{i}_{v})
has components being Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables and
\widetilde{z}^{(i)}_{\mathsf{n}}
are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables. By the bottom to top overall induction, we can assume that all
\widetilde{z}^{(i)}_{\mathsf{s}}
are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables.

For this (left to right) induction on
i
, the base case
i=0
is established in the paragraph above:
\zeta_{dh}(e_{v})
has components that are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables by Equation (
21
). Let us proceed with the inductive step, assuming that the components of
Y_{i}:=Y_{e^{i}_{v}}=\zeta_{dh}(e^{i}_{v})
and
\widetilde{z}^{(i+1)}_{\mathsf{s}}
are all Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables. We want to show that the associated
\widetilde{z}^{i+1}_{\mathsf{n}}
, satisfying Condition (5), is a Laurent polynomial in the
u
-variables and that so are the components of
Y_{i+1}:=\zeta_{dh}(e^{i+1}_{v})
. First, note that the element
\xi_{j}(Y_{i})
is a Laurent polynomial in the
u
-variables in this case, where
j\in\mathsf{D}
is the color of the solid edges north and south of
v_{i}
. Indeed, this holds because both the components of
Y_{i}
and
\widetilde{z}^{(i+1)}_{\mathsf{s}}
are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables, by the induction hypothesis. Second, since we must satisfy Condition (5), we must have

\widetilde{z}^{(i+1)}_{\mathsf{n}}=\widetilde{z}^{(i+1)}_{\mathsf{s}}-\xi_{j}(Y_{i}),

and it follows that
\widetilde{z}^{(i+1)}_{\mathsf{n}}
is a Laurent polynomial in the
u
-variables, as this holds for both summands
\widetilde{z}^{(i+1)}_{\mathsf{s}}
and
-\xi_{j}(Y_{i})
. Finally, it suffices to argue that the components of
Y_{i+1}
are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables. This follows from the third equation of Condition (5):

Y_{i+1}=(B_{j}(\widetilde{z}^{i+1}_{\mathsf{s}})\chi_{j}(u^{i+1}_{\mathsf{s}}))^{-1}\cdot Y_{i}\cdot(B_{j}(\widetilde{z}^{i+1}_{\mathsf{n}})\chi_{j}(u^{i+1}_{\mathsf{n}})).

Indeed, all entries on the right hand side have components that are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables, and thus this also holds for their product. This completes the left-to-right induction on
i
and concludes the inductive step of the bottom-to-top induction in the case that we are crossing a trivalent vertex.

(3)

If we cross a hexavalent vertex, then the inductive assumption is that
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{sw}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{se}}
are Laurent polynomials in the
u
-variables. Then Condition (3) implies that
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{n}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}
are Laurent polynomials in
u
-variables, as required.

(4)

The case of crossing a tetravalent vertex is immediate, as the labels do not change.

Finally, we must verify that the variables
\{z_{i}\}
define a point in
X(\beta)
. For that, we note that this inductive process leads to a collection of framed flags with
\mathsf{U}
on the leftmost region, and a framed version of
\delta(\beta)\mathsf{B}
on the top rightmost region. By Condition
(1)
in Definition
5.8
, this defines a point in
X(\beta)
.
∎

To conclude this subsection, we emphasize the following two properties of framed weaves
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
, entirely to do with solid edges and vertices of
\mathfrak{W}
itself:

(i)

Near a trivalent or tetravalent vertex, the values of the labeling
\zeta
for the (solid) edges above the vertex uniquely determine the values of the labeling for the edges below the vertex. That is, for a trivalent vertex,
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{nw}})
and
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{ne}})
uniquely determine
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{s}})
. This is depicted in Figure
14
. Similarly, for a tetravalent vertex,
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{nw}})
and
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{ne}})
uniquely determine
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{se}})
, and
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{sw}})
.

(ii)

For a hexavalent vertex, the values of the labeling
\zeta
for the (solid) edges above the vertex do
not
uniquely determine the values of the labeling for the (solid) edges below the vertex. Nevertheless, they determine them up to a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
choice. For instance,
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{nw}}),\zeta(e_{\mathsf{n}}),\zeta(e_{\mathsf{ne}})
together with the value
u_{\mathsf{s}}
do determine
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{sw}}),\zeta(e_{\mathsf{s}})
and
\zeta(e_{\mathsf{se}})
. This indicates that having a defining rule for the
u
-variables will potentially allow for the construction of a unique framed weave via a propagation argument, from the top of the weave to its bottom. This is indeed what occurs in the proof of Theorem
5.12
, where the (to be) cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables will uniquely specify a framed weave by determining the
u
-variables.

In other words, by item (ii) above, a weave
\mathfrak{W}
often underlies many framed weaves
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
, i.e. the choice of
\zeta
is not unique.

5.1.5.
Independence of choices for raked weaves

Given a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}
, we can perform a compactly supported isotopy that moves a trivalent vertex upwards (or downwards). The resulting Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}^{\prime}
is effectively the same: the planar graphs
\mathfrak{W}
and
\mathfrak{W}^{\prime}
are identical. That said, the associated raked weaves
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
and
(\mathfrak{W}^{\prime})^{=}
might differ by a sliding of dashed yellow lines through vertices of the weave. We want constructions to be independent of whether we have started with
\mathfrak{W}
or
\mathfrak{W}^{\prime}
, i.e. independent of the exact heights of the vertices of
\mathfrak{W}
. This independence was essentially established in
[
17
, Section 5]
. We include the necessary details here for completeness and refer to
[
17
, Section 5.2.1]
for further discussions:

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
v\in\mathfrak{W}
be a vertex in a Demazure weave. Consider a dashed yellow line
y_{\mathsf{n}}
, resp.
y_{\mathsf{s}}
, right above the vertex
v
, resp. below. Then all the
(\widetilde{z},u)
-variables above
y_{\mathsf{n}}
and below
v
coincide with the corresponding
(\widetilde{z},u)
-variables above
v
and below
y_{\mathsf{s}}
, i.e. they are independent of whether we choose the dashed yellow line above or below the vertex.

Proof.

There are three cases to verify, depending on the type of vertex
v\in\mathfrak{W}
.

(1)

If
v
is a trivalent vertex of color
i\in\mathsf{D}
, we use Identity (
12
) in the proof of Lemma 4.2. Indeed, let us set
a=c=1
in that identity and observe that
z_{2}
does not change. Then Identity (
12
) implies that the variable
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}
is independent of whether we are using the dashed yellow line
y_{\mathsf{n}}
or
y_{\mathsf{s}}
. If we are using the dashed line
y_{\mathsf{n}}
above
v
, we have the following changes of variables:

(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}},u_{\mathsf{nw}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}},u_{\mathsf{ne}})\to(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}+\xi_{i}(Y),u_{\mathsf{nw}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}},u_{\mathsf{ne}})\to(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}+\xi_{i}(Y)-u_{\mathsf{nw}}^{-2}\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{ne}}).

If we are instead using the dashed line
y_{\mathsf{s}}
below
v
, then we obtain

(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}},u_{\mathsf{nw}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}},u_{\mathsf{ne}})\to(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}-u_{\mathsf{nw}}^{-2}\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{ne}})\to(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}+\xi_{i}(Y)-u_{\mathsf{nw}}^{-2}\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{ne}}).

Let us now study how the unipotent-valued labelings of dashed edges change. On the one hand, if the dashed line is
y_{\mathsf{n}}
, above the trivalent vertex, then the dashed edge to the right of
e_{\mathsf{n}\mathsf{e}}
will be labeled by
Y^{\prime\prime}
, where
Y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{U}
is defined by the identity

(22)

YB_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{nw}})B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{ne}})=B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}+\xi_{i}(Y))\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{nw}})B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{ne}})Y^{\prime\prime}.

Here we used, just as in the previous paragraph, that the variable
\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}
is the same as the
\widetilde{z}
-variable on the solid edge continuing
e_{\mathsf{ne}}
upwards just above
y_{\mathsf{n}}
.
On the other hand, if the dashed line is
y_{\mathsf{s}}
, below the trivalent vertex, then the dashed edge to the right of
e_{\mathsf{s}}
will be labeled by
Y^{\prime}
, where
Y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{U}
is defined by

(23)

YB_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}-u_{\mathsf{nw}}^{-2}\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1})\chi_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{ne}})=B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}-u_{\mathsf{nw}}^{-2}\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1}+\xi_{i}(Y))\chi_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}u_{\mathsf{nw}}u_{\mathsf{ne}})Y^{\prime}.

By applying identity (
20
) twice, first to the left hand sides of (
22
) and (
23
), and then to their right hand sides, we obtain that

Y^{\prime}\varphi_{i}\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1}u_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-2}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)=\varphi_{i}\left(\begin{matrix}1&-\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-1}u_{\mathsf{ne}}^{-2}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)Y^{\prime\prime}.

This implies that the following two situations for a (vertical) solid edge, to the right of the trivalent vertex and disjoint from it, lead to the same variables above and below. First, a solid edge first crosses the dashed edge emanating from the trivalent vertex and then the dashed edge labeled by
Y^{\prime}
. Second, this same solid edge first crosses the dashed line labeled by
Y^{\prime\prime}
and then the dashed line emanating from the trivalent vertex. Thus, the variables attached to solid edges to the right of this trivalent vertex and below both dashed edges do not depend on the relative position of the dashed edges, as required.

(2)

For a hexavalent vertex, using notation analogous to item (1) above, the statement follows from the identities

YB_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{nw}})B_{j}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{n}})\chi_{j}(u_{\mathsf{n}})B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{ne}})=

B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}}^{\prime})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{nw}})B_{j}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{n}}^{\prime})\chi_{j}(u_{\mathsf{n}})B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}}^{\prime})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{ne}})Y^{\prime}=

B_{j}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{sw}})\chi_{j}(u_{\mathsf{sw}})B_{i}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathsf{s}})B_{j}(\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{se}}^{\prime})\chi_{j}(u_{\mathsf{se}})Y^{\prime}

and the fact that all the variables (and
Y^{\prime}
) are uniquely determined by the variables

Y,\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{nw}},u_{\mathsf{nw}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{n}},u_{\mathsf{n}},\widetilde{z}_{\mathsf{ne}},u_{\mathsf{ne}},u_{\mathsf{sw}},u_{\mathsf{s}},u_{\mathsf{se}}.

(3)

The case of a tetravalent vertex is similar.

∎

5.2.
Cluster variables in Demazure weaves

Given a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{W}
, let
\gamma_{v}:E(\mathfrak{W})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be the cycles associated to the trivalent vertices
v\in\mathfrak{W}
as in Definition
4.13
. Let us now construct a collection of functions
\{A_{v}\}
on
X(\beta)
, a priori rational, that will later be proven to
be cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables
in a cluster seed for the regular ring of functions
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
. Such functions are indexed by the trivalent vertices
v\in\mathfrak{W}
of the given Demazure weave. They are constructed recursively and, simultaneously, determine a framed weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{W}
be a Demazure weave for a positive braid word
\beta
and
\mathfrak{W}_{3}
its set of trivalent vertices. Then there exists a unique collection

\{A_{v}\}_{v\in\mathfrak{W}_{3}}
of rational functions
A_{v}:=A_{v}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{\ell})\in\mathbb{C}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{\ell})
, indexed by the trivalent vertices
v\in\mathfrak{W}_{3}
, and a unique labeling
\zeta
of
\mathfrak{W}
such that

(i)

The
u
-variable
u_{e}
of the label
\zeta_{sd}(e)
,
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
, is given by

(24)

u_{e}=\prod_{v}A_{v}^{\gamma_{v}(e)}.

(ii)

The labeled weave
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is a framed weave. That is, this assignment of
u
-variables,
\widetilde{z}
-variables, and the dashed labels
\zeta_{dh}
of
\zeta
satisfy all the conditions in Definition
5.8
.

Proof.

The construction of the rational functions

\{A_{v}\}_{v\in\mathfrak{W}_{3}}
is achieved by simultaneously building the labeling
\zeta
such that
(\mathfrak{W},\zeta)
is a framed weave. The key principles in the construction are:

(1)

The labeling
\zeta
at the top (solid) edges of the weave
e\in E_{sd}(\mathfrak{W})
is determined from the start by virtue of Condition (1) in Definition
5.8
of a framed weave. That is, we declare
\zeta(e_{i}):=(z_{i},1)
if
e_{i}
is the solid weave edge corresponding to the
i
-th crossing of
\beta
, starting at the northern boundary of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
.
Note that
\gamma_{v}(e_{i})=0
for all Lusztig cycles, which is compatible with the condition
u_{e_{i}}=1
.

(2)

Both
\{A_{v}\}_{v}
and
\zeta
are then built by scanning down the weave
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
top to bottom. Namely, for each vertex
\vartheta
of the weave

\mathfrak{W}
, we consider two horizontal slices
H^{\pm}_{\vartheta}
of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
so that
H^{+}_{\vartheta}
is above the vertex
\vartheta
,
H^{-}_{\vartheta}
is below the vertex
\vartheta
and there are no other vertices of
\mathfrak{W}^{=}
(except for
\vartheta
) in the closed strip region between
H^{+}_{\vartheta}
and
H^{-}_{\vartheta}
. We will construct
\{A_{v}\}_{v}
and
\zeta
inductively, by assuming that we have constructed them for all edges above (and intersecting)
H^{+}_{\vartheta}
and then propagating it down to the edges that intersect
H^{-}_{\vartheta}
